PREFACE 

The materials contained in the present volume form a continu- 
ation of previous series of Kwakiutl texts published by me. 

The history of the collection, the methods of recording and of 
publication require some explanation. 

I have set forth the general development of my work on the 
Pacific Coast in the introduction to the description of Kwakiutl 
material culture in Vol. X of the Publications of the Jesup Expe- 
dition. 

The first texts collected by me were a number of songs recorded 
_in 1886. At that time I was under the somewhat naive impression 
that the missionaries who had translated parts of the New Testa- 
ment were fully conversant with the language. My journey of that 
year was for the purpose of a general reconnoissance and for 
collecting Museum specimens. For this reason not much time 
could be given to linguistic studies for which I was, furthermore, 
ill prepared. Some prose texts were collected in 1893 during the 
World’s Columbian Expedition, but the strenuous duties of my 
position as chief assistant of the Departement of Anthropology, 
did not give much time for research. During the Exposition I 
began to teach Mr. George Hunt, who was in Chicago in charge of 
a number of Kwakiutl Indians, to write Kwakiutl, his mother 
tongue. Although the son of a Tlingit mother and of a Scotch 
father, he had grown up at Fort Rupert among the Kwakiutl, and 
-their language is his mother tongue. During my various visits to 
British Columbia undertaken on behalf of the Committee of the 
British Association for the Advancement of Science, appointed 
for the study of the North-Western tribes of Canada, opportunities 
for linguistic study were limited, because according to specific 
instructions by Horatio Hale, Editor of the Committee, linguistic 
work was to be confined to vocabularies and very brief grammatical 
sketches in the style of his pioneer work done for the Wilkes 
Expedition. In 1895 my time was so entirely taken up with obser- 
ving and recording the winter ceremonial then in progress, that 
no advance was made in the study of the language. I merely 
succeeded in writing, as well as I could, the songs and brief formulas 
of the ceremonial. In 1900 I had a somewhat better chance to 
study the language in connection with the work of the Jesup 
Expedition. 

xX Religion of the Kwakwutl 

In 1903 Mr. Hunt visited New York to arrange the collections 
made by him and by myself for the American Museum of Natural 
History and I was able to improve his own writing and my own 
hearing, but not as thoroughly as desired. It seemed then that no 
future opportunity to revisit Vancouver Island would present 
itself and I decided to publish the texts which Mr. Hunt had 
written, in answer to my requests and to specific questions, between 
1895—1900. I may repeat here the remarks in regard to the texts, 
stated in the introduction to Volume III of the Publications of the 
Jesup Expedition: 

‘In 1893 Mr. Hunt began to record material in the Kwakiutl language 
according to the system of spelling adopted by me. On repeated visits to 
British Columbia I had an opportunity to critically discuss with him the 
material he had written, which resulted in constant improvement in his 
method of writing and in my knowledge of the phonetics of the language. 
I believe that the system of sounds adopted in the present volume represents 
the complete series of sounds that occur in the Kwakiutl language. 

The records made by Mr. Hunt were transmitted to me from time to 
time. After I had made a study of the texts, I revised them critically, with 
the assistance of Mr. Hunt, in regard to both phonetics and grammar. 
This work was done during my visits to British Columbia in 1897 and 1900. 
Furthermore, the texts were critically gone over by me with the assistance 
of Mr. Wiliam Brotchie of Alert Bay, a half-blood Nimkish (one of the 
tribes of the Kwakiutl), who made the translation of the Gospels for the Rev. 
Alfred J. Hall. Wherever there was a discrepancy of opinion in regard to 
construction between Mr. Brotchie and Mr. Hunt, I have taken pains to 
investigate the doubtful points. 

At the present time the phonetics of Kwakiutl are uncertain. This is 
particularly true of the pronunciation of 6, which is pronounced by Mr. 
Brotchie and many young men like é, while Mr. Hunt and many of the older 
people pronounce this sound almost like é1. The same uncertainty prevails 
in regard to terminal sounds. Many of the Indians modify terminal sound 
according to the initial sounds of the following word; while others, par- 
ticularly when speaking slowly, do not admit such influence. It is possible 
that the slowness of reading and writing these texts has eliminated a con- 
siderable number of cases in which the terminal sound of a word ought to be 
modified by the initial sound of the following word. There is particularly a 
considerable discrepancy of opinion as to the propriety of modifying the 
terminal t and terminal k‘, which, when followed by a consonant, are 
pronounced by many people I and x’.” 

I had no opportunity to revisit the native villages on the coast 
of British Columbia until 1923 when I went to Bella Bella for a 
study of the northern dialect of the Kwakiutl language. Mr. Hunt 
served me there as interpreter and I continued to work with him 
whenever no Bella Bella informants were available. Twice more, in 
1922 and 1927, I met Mr. Hunt for a short time in Victoria, British 
Columbia. In the latter year I corrected from his dictation pp. 160 
to 162 of the present volume. I believe these are practically correct. 

Preface XI 

Although Mr. Hunt’s writing has considerably improved, there 
are still doubts in regard to many points. Perhaps the most serious 
defect of his writing is the confusion between 4 and 4 or E (o). 
I believe the later sound is by origin a short 4 which is so much 
weakened that it takes its timber from the adjoining consonants. 
After palatized k sounds it becomes i (1), after labialized sounds 
u (v). Since the ti is generally pronounced with very full breath, 
Mr. Hunt writes generally we. Owing to the uncertainty of the use 
of 4 there are many cases in which it is doubtful whether we should 
read wa or U (wE). Another serious difficulty is presented by the 
unusually large number of glottal stops. Mr. Hunt does not 
distinguish clearly between voicing and glottal stops. Undoubtedly 
every initial vowel begins with a glottal stop. I believe all endings 
expressing invisibility (see F. Boas Sketch of the Kwakiutl Gram- 
mar, Handbook of American Indian Languages, Bulletin 40, 
Bureau of American Ethnology, pp. 527 et seq.) also end in glottal 
stops. The qa® expressing final clauses has a glottal stop and is 
followed by a verbal form ending in a glottal stop. In regard to all 
these, great irregularities will be found in the printed texts. In 
some cases the stops are given, in others not. I must apologize for 
these inconsistencies which are due to my own lack of definite 
information. Equally uncertain are the initial glottal stops before 
m,n, w, y, andl. There is also uncertainty in regard to the use of 
& and o and the use of o, u on the one hand, and e, i on the other. 
The timber of the vowel depends to a great extent upon adjoining 
consonants. Each of the pairsie and o wu is evidently etymologi- 
cally a single sound. In some passages Mr. Hunt substituted 4 or E 
for U. 

While on the whole the grammatical structure seems to corres- 
pond to older Kwakiutl usage, there are undoubtedly numerous 
places in which Mr. Hunt, probably on account of the slowness of 
writing lost the syntactic connection. In his manuscripts passages 
are found in which the subject is expressed in objective form. 
When confronted with the fact he acknowledged that he had made 
a mistake. I have not ventured to make many changes because 
on the whole errors are not frequent although certain errors are 
liable to occur in the same passage a number of times, other 
consistent errors in other passages. 

When the publication of the various collections of texts was 
started there seemed to be no prospect of a careful revision and 
‘since their value is as much ethnological and stylistic as linguistic, 
it seemed best to publish them notwithstanding their imperfections. 
In too many cases material of great value has been lost because the 

XII 

Religion of the Kwakvutl 

author waited to perfect and complete it and the unpublished 
manuscripts are lost to science. I present my material fully cons- 
cious of its shortcomings. 

It is also regrettable that the bulk of the material has been 
obtained from one single informant. This leaves us in doubt 
whether we are dealing with individual or with tribal style. The 
collection contained in the Columbia University Contributions to 
Anthropology, Vol. IJ, is so far the only material that can be used 
for the comparison of styles of various persons.

In order to show the character of the changes which I have 
made in Mr. Hunt’s writing, I give in the following an accurate 
copy of what he has written, my transcription, and translation. 

XIV Religion of the Kwakiutl 

It will be noticed that Mr. Hunt divides words in a manner 
different from my own. In many cases the division of his words 
corresponds to a glottal stop in a word. Furthermore, he writes all 
the prenominal elements, such as xa and sa, as independent words 
while I join them to the preceding word on account of the close 
phonetic association which brings it about that, for imstance, a 
word ending in s and followed by sa will change to tsa. [tis however 
true that under certain conditions xa and sa may be initial 
elements of a clause. Owing to an early misunderstanding Mr. 
Hunt uses tu for L and vice versa.

1 T recorded this song in 1892 directly from the singing of an Indian. 
Later Mr. John C. Fillmore recorded it independently from a phonographic 
cylinder which I had made. The present version agrees with the words of 
the earlier one and was written down by Mr. Hunt in 1926. The tune has 
been published in “Supplement to vol. [X, Internationales Archiv fur Ethno- 
graphie, Leyden‘‘, 1896, p. 7.

Social Organization and Secret. Societies of the Kwakiutl Indians, An. Rep.
